\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -23.62 -19.69 M -23.62 -19.69 L -23.13 -19.48 L -22.84 -18.8 L -22.49 -18.24 L -22.05 -17.91 L -21.51 -17.83 L -20.87 -17.98 L -20.18 -18.25 L -19.49 -18.52 L -18.85 -18.67 L -18.3 -18.59 L -17.86 -18.26 L -17.52 -17.7 L -17.22 -17.02 L -16.93 -16.34 L -16.59 -15.78 L -16.15 -15.45 L -15.6 -15.37 L -14.96 -15.52 L -14.27 -15.79 L -13.58 -16.06 L -12.94 -16.21 L -12.4 -16.13 L -11.96 -15.8 L -11.61 -15.24 L -11.32 -14.56 L -11.03 -13.87 L -10.68 -13.32 L -10.24 -12.99 L -9.7 -12.91 L -9.06 -13.05 L -8.37 -13.33 L -7.67 -13.6 L -7.04 -13.75 L -6.49 -13.67 L -6.05 -13.34 L -5.71 -12.78 L -5.41 -12.1 L -5.12 -11.41 L -4.77 -10.86 L -4.34 -10.53 L -3.79 -10.45 L -3.15 -10.59 L -2.46 -10.87 L -1.77 -11.14 L -1.13 -11.29 L -0.59 -11.21 L -0 -9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}+\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}+\bm{k}-\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2})\Big[\kappa(\varepsilon\cdot p_{1})^{2}\braket{p_{2}^{\prime}|p_{2}}+\kappa(\varepsilon\cdot p_{2})^{2}\braket{p_{1}^{\prime}|p_{1}}\Big],

(3.15)

T_{c}

\displaystyle=-\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}^{\prime}_{1}+\bm{p}^{\prime}_{2}+\bm{k}-\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2})\mathcal{A}_{\bm{3},\bm{2}}=\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{3})\,.

(3.16)

Here,
\varepsilon_{\mu}=\varepsilon_{\mu}(k)
is the polarisation vector with the polarisation being implicit, and
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{3},\bm{2}}
is an off-energy-shell extension of the 2-to-3 amplitude.
6
6

6

The overall minus sign is due to the different sign conventions of the
T
-matrix and the amplitudes:
S_{\beta,\alpha}=\braket{\beta|\alpha}-i\hat{\rm\delta}(E_{\alpha}-E_{\beta})T_{\beta,\alpha}=\braket{\beta|\alpha}+i\hat{\rm\delta}^{(4)}(p_{\alpha}-p_{\beta})\mathcal{A}_{\beta,\alpha}
We stress that the
T
-matrix is defined after removing the energy conservation (see (
2.3
)), and only has the delta functions for the momentum conservation. The three-point does not vanish even for the real kinematics. Hence, the
T
-matrix solely comes from the disconnected part at the leading order in
\kappa=\sqrt{32\pi G}
. The radiation potential agrees with the
T
-matrix at the leading order, yielding

Leading-order radiation potential in momentum space

\displaystyle R_{\rm PM}=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2\omega}}\left[\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}-\bm{p}_{2})\frac{(\varepsilon\cdot p_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{\sqrt{4E_{1}E_{1}^{\prime}}}+\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}-\bm{p}_{1})\frac{(\varepsilon\cdot p_{2}^{\prime})^{2}}{\sqrt{4E_{2}E_{2}^{\prime}}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{3})\,.

(3.17)

3.2
Effective potentials to wavefunctions

Having obtained the effective potentials through the Born subtractions, the next problem is solving the Schrödinger equations (
2.28
) and (
2.35
). Since we are interested in the classical regime, the powerful method to compute a non-perturbative wavefunction is the WKB approximation.

We factorise the two-particle states into the centre-of-mass (COM) motion and relative motion states

Two-particle states

\displaystyle\ket{p_{1};p_{2}}=\sqrt{4E_{1}E_{2}}\ket{\bm{P}}\otimes\ket{\bm{p}}\,,\qquad\ket{\hat{\alpha}\Psi^{\pm}_{12}}=\sqrt{4E_{1}E_{2}}\ket{\bm{P}}\otimes\ket{\Psi^{\pm}_{\bm{p}}}

(3.18)

with the non-relativistic normalisations for “bold” states

\displaystyle\braket{\bm{P}^{\prime}|\bm{P}}=\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{P}^{\prime}-\bm{P})\,,\qquad\braket{\bm{p}^{\prime}|\bm{p}}=\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}^{\prime}-\bm{p})\,.

(3.19)

The total and relative momenta are defined by

\displaystyle\bm{P}=\bm{p}_{1}+\bm{p}_{2}\,,\qquad\bm{p}=\eta_{2}\bm{p}_{1}-\eta_{1}\bm{p}_{2}\,,

(3.20)

with
\eta_{1}(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})+\eta_{2}(\bm{p}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})=1
. Note that there is no unique notion of relativistic COM. This ambiguity usually does not enter into the computation of the 2-to-2 amplitude by working in the COM frame
\bm{P}=\bm{p}_{1}+\bm{p}_{2}=0
, where
\bm{p}=\bm{p}_{1}=-\bm{p}_{2}
irrespectively of the choice of
\eta_{a}
. In the present paper, however, we eventually require a deviation from the COM frame due to the presence of graviton. We leave
\eta_{a}
undecided for a while, and will choose it for our convenience later.

In our case, we should also deal with the three-particle states. While the COM motion of the three particles can be factored out, the remaining relative motion is still a two-body state, which cannot be easily solved when interacting. However, the gravitational force between the graviton and massive bodies should be much weaker than the force between the massive bodies. The relative motion state may be approximately factorised into the relative motion of the massive bodies and the motion of the graviton, a la the Born-Oppenheimer approximation. Therefore, our three-particle state may be approximated by

Three-particle states

\displaystyle\ket{p_{1};p_{2};k}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{8E_{1}E_{2}\omega}

\displaystyle~~~\ket{\bm{P}+\bm{k}}

\displaystyle~~\otimes~~~~~~\ket{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle\otimes~~~~\ket{\bm{k}}\,,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\ket{\hat{\beta}\psi^{-}_{12k}}

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm BO}}{=}\sqrt{8E_{1}E_{2}\omega}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\ket{\bm{P}+\bm{k}}}_{\text{3-particle COM}}

\displaystyle~~\otimes\underbrace{\ket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{2-body}\\
\text{relative motion}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\otimes~~\underbrace{\ket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}}}_{\text{graviton}}\,.

(3.22)

There are a few comments. The second factorisation is expected to hold in the classical limit, where we have a large hierarchy between the massive and massless momenta,
p_{a},p_{a}^{\prime}\gg k
(we use “
\overset{{\rm BO}}{=}
” for equalities under (
3.22
) to keep track of using this factorisation assumption). Nevertheless, the graviton momentum
\bm{k}
should
not
be neglected in the COM state of the three bodies
\ket{\bm{P}+\bm{k}}
, as we will see shortly and in Sec.
4
. Next, since the two-body potential of
\ket{\hat{\beta}\psi^{-}_{12k}}
coincides with the conjugate of the two-body potential
\hat{V}_{\rm PM}
, the relative state of 1 and 2 is the same as the conjugate of the one in (
3.18
). Finally, we retain the general scattering state
\ket{\Psi^{\pm}_{\bm{k}}}
to see the structures of amplitudes and observables, although we have not derived the Schrödinger equation for this. When necessary, we shall simply approximate it to be a free state
\ket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}}=\ket{\bm{k}}
.

All in all, our problem is to find the interacting state of the relative motion states
\ket{\Psi^{\pm}_{\bm{p}}}
. Using (
3.18
), eq. (
3.12
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle\braket{\bm{P}^{\prime};\bm{p}^{\prime}|\hat{V}_{\rm PM}|\bm{P};\bm{p}}=\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{P}^{\prime}-\bm{P})V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{p};\bm{P})\,,

(3.23)

and the Schrödinger equation (
2.28
) yields

\displaystyle(E_{12}-E_{12}^{\prime})\braket{\bm{p}^{\prime}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}-\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\rho}\,V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{\rho};\bm{P})\braket{\bm{\rho}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}=0\,.

(3.24)

Here, the potential can depend on the total momentum
\bm{P}
because we have not assumed the COM frame. Note that the Schrödinger equation for the out state reads

\displaystyle(E_{12}-E_{12}^{\prime})\braket{\bm{p}^{\prime}|\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}}}-\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\rho}\,V^{*}_{\rm PM}(\bm{\rho},\bm{p}^{\prime};\bm{P})\braket{\bm{\rho}|\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}}}=0\,,

(3.25)

with the different boundary condition, but the way to find the solution is formally the same as the in state (and only the in state is needed to compute the waveform in the end). Therefore, we only consider the in state (
3.24
) in the following.

Let us move to the position space. The position-space potential is defined by the Fourier transform
[
13
]

\displaystyle V_{\rm PM}(\bar{\bm{p}},\bm{x};\bm{P})

\displaystyle:=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}\,e^{-i\bm{q}\cdot\bm{x}}V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{p};\bm{P})|_{\bm{p}=\bar{\bm{p}}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{q},\bm{p}^{\prime}=\bar{\bm{p}}-\frac{1}{2}\bm{q}}\,,

(3.26)

with
\bm{q}:=\bm{p}-\bm{p}^{\prime}
being the momentum transfer and
\bar{\bm{p}}:=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{p}+\bm{p}^{\prime})
.
7
7

7

With this definition, the position-space potential becomes real when it is Hermitian
\hat{V}_{\rm PM}=\hat{V}_{\rm PM}^{\dagger}
. In Section
4
, we will assume a Hermitian potential, but we do not make that assumption in this section.
The position-space Schrödinger equation is obtained by performing the Fourier transform of (
3.24
) with respect to
\bm{p}^{\prime}
. Notice that

\displaystyle\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\rho}\,V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{\rho};\bm{P})e^{i\bm{p}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{x}}\braket{\bm{\rho}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\rho}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}\,V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{\rho};\bm{P})e^{i\bm{p}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{x}-i\bm{\rho}\cdot\bm{x}^{\prime}}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bar{\bm{p}}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bar{\bm{p}}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}\,e^{-i\bm{q}\cdot\bm{x}^{\prime}+\frac{i}{2}\bm{q}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}V_{\rm PM}(\bar{\bm{p}},\bm{q};\bm{P})

(3.27)

where
\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})=\braket{\bm{x}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}
is the wavefunction in the position space. To write this integral in terms of (
3.26
), we expand
e^{\frac{i}{2}\bm{q}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}
and rewrite the series by using the derivatives:

(
3.27
)

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bar{\bm{p}}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bar{\bm{p}}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}e^{-i\bm{q}\cdot\bm{x}^{\prime}}\left[1+\frac{i}{2}\bm{q}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})+\cdots\right]V_{\rm PM}(\bar{\bm{p}},\bm{q};\bm{P})

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bar{\bm{p}}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bar{\bm{p}}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\left[1-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})\cdot\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}+\cdots\right]V_{\rm PM}(\bar{\bm{p}},\bm{x}^{\prime};\bm{P})

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bar{\bm{p}}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bar{\bm{p}}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\left[1+\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\bar{\bm{p}}}\cdot\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}+\cdots\right]V_{\rm PM}(\bar{\bm{p}},\bm{x}^{\prime};\bm{P})\,.

(3.28)

We have replaced
e^{-i\bar{\bm{p}}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})
with
i\partial_{\bar{\bm{p}}}e^{-i\bar{\bm{p}}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}
and then performed the integration by parts to obtain the last equation. Therefore, the Schrödinger equation in the position space is given by, after relabelling the integration variable
\bar{\bm{p}}\to\bm{p}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle E_{12}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})-\int{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}\,e^{-i\bm{p}^{\prime}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\left[1+\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}+\cdots\right]H_{12}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{x}^{\prime};\bm{P})=0\,.

(3.29)

with the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{12}(\bm{p},\bm{x};\bm{P})=\sqrt{m_{1}^{2}+\bm{p}_{1}^{2}}+\sqrt{m_{2}^{2}+\bm{p}_{2}^{2}}+V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p},\bm{x};\bm{P})\,.

(3.30)

From (
3.29
), one may obtain the familiar differential form of the Schrödinger equation by replacing the
\bm{p}^{\prime}
-dependence of the Hamiltonian according to
\bm{p}^{\prime}\to\hat{\bm{p}}^{\prime}=-i\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}
and performing the
\bm{x}^{\prime}
and
\bm{p}^{\prime}
integrals (see the end of Appendix
A
). We, however, work in the integral form (
3.29
) because the Hamiltonian
H_{12}
is a non-linear function of
\bm{p}
and thus the differential form is not practically useful.

We solve the Schrödinger equation (
3.29
) under the WKB approximation. Reintroducing
\hbar
, eq. (
3.29
) becomes

\displaystyle E_{12}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})-\int\frac{{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{3}}\,e^{-i\bm{p}^{\prime}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})/\hbar}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\left[1+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\partial_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}+\cdots\right]H_{12}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{x}^{\prime};\bm{P})=0\,.

(3.31)

Let us consider the WKB ansatz

\displaystyle\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})=A_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}\,,

(3.32)

and evaluate the integral in (
3.31
) around the saddle

\displaystyle\bm{x}=\bm{x}^{\prime}\,,\qquad\bm{p}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})}{\partial\bm{x}^{\prime}}=:\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,.

(3.33)

The saddle-point approximation up to the sub-leading order yields

\displaystyle E_{12}(\bm{p};\bm{P})-H_{12}(\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}),\bm{x};\bm{P})

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.34)

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{x}}\cdot\left[\bm{v}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x})A_{\bm{p}}^{2}(\bm{x})\right]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.35)

with
\bm{v}_{\rm bulk}:=\left.\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial\bm{p}}\right|_{\bm{p}=\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x})}
. Solving the second equation under the boundary condition that
A_{\bm{p}}\to 1
as the free wave limit
\sigma_{\bm{p}}\to\bm{p}\cdot\bm{x}
, we find
8
8

8

We assume that
\sigma_{\bm{p}}
satisfies the boundary condition for scattering in states. If one is interested in the emission from a bound state, one should instead use the bound state wavefunction by changing the boundary condition.

WKB wavefunction

\displaystyle\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})=\sqrt{\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}\,e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}\,.

(3.36)

The detailed derivation of (
3.34
)-(
3.36
) is described in Appendix
A
. Since (
3.34
) is the classical Hamilton-Jacobi equation in the
bulk
, the quantity
\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x})=\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})
in (
3.34
) can interpreted as the
bulk
momentum in the classical limit (and
\sigma_{\bm{p}}
is Hamilton’s characteristic function).
9
9

9

However, this interpretation requires care because our potential is not necessarily real. An imaginary part of the two-body potential
V_{\rm PM}
, even if it is
\hbar
suppressed, will lead to an imaginary part of
\sigma_{\bm{p}}
, which changes the overall size of the wavefunction.

Note that (
3.34
)-(
3.36
) arise from a saddle-point approximation applied to a Hamiltonian system with a fully relativistic kinetic term and potential. It has been shown that such a system can be recast into a non-relativistic–looking form by quantising, rather than the Hamiltonian itself, the solution in phase space. This observation was first made at leading order in
[
78
]
, later conjectured to hold to all orders in
[
10
]
, and subsequently proven in
[
79
,
80
]
. One advantage of this approach is that
\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}^{2}(\bm{x})
in isotropic coordinates is proportional to the finite part of a 2-to-2 amplitude to all orders.
10
10

10

This relation holds only in
D=4
, as it already fails at one loop for
D>4
. For a proof, see Section 4 of
[
81
]
.
Here, instead, we returned to the relativistic form to determine the wavefunction in generic coordinates, including the normalisation
A_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})
, which is crucial during wave generation.

The analysis so far has not assumed any specific coordinates. We wish to assume the COM frame to simplify (
3.34
). However, even if we take the COM frame initially
\bm{P}=0
, the state evolves into the three-particle state with the momentum conservation
\bm{P}^{\prime}+\bm{k}=\bm{P}
, meaning that the two bodies cannot remain in the COM frame. As for massless particles, what we keep finite in the classical limit is the wavenumber
\bar{\bm{k}}=\bm{k}/\hbar
, so the deviation from the COM frame is
\hbar
-suppressed. Let us thus solve (
3.34
) with
\bm{P}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
(or the corresponding equation for the out state). The solutions should be given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\bm{p}}=\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}+\hbar\,\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}

(3.37)

where
\sigma_{\bm{p}}^{\rm COM}
is the solutions in the COM frame
\bm{P}=0
. Looking at the WKB wavefunction (
3.36
), one sees that the correction to the phase
\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}
will give a
finite
contribution even in the classical limit
\hbar\to 0
. This is precisely the reason why we must be cautious of deviations from the COM frame. In Sec.
4
, we will see that
\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})
is indeed necessary to reproduce the classical wave emission from worldlines.

We now fix the definition of the COM. A convenient choice is

\displaystyle\eta_{1}=\frac{E_{1}}{E_{12}}\,,\qquad\eta_{2}=\frac{E_{2}}{E_{12}}

(3.38)

where the COM position
\bm{X}
conjugate to the total momentum remains constant in the COM frame when the interaction is turned off, i.e.,
E_{1}(\bm{p}_{1})+E_{2}(\bm{p}_{2})=E_{1}(\bm{p})+E_{2}(-\bm{p})+\mathcal{O}(\bm{P}^{2})
. The equation for
\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}
is then found to be

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\,\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}}{\partial\bm{x}}\cdot\left.\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}}\right|_{\bm{P}=0}+\bm{V}_{\rm bulk}\cdot\bm{P}=0\,,

(3.39)

where

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}):=\left.\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial\bm{P}}\right|_{\bm{P}=0}=\left.\frac{\partial V_{\rm PM}}{\partial\bm{P}}\right|_{\bm{P}=0}

(3.40)

is understood as the bulk velocity of the COM position
\bm{X}
in the classical limit.
11
11

11

The COM frame
\bm{P}=0
does not necessarily mean
\bm{V}_{\rm bulk}=0
in relativistic problems.
We leave a further discussion of (
3.39
) until Sec.
4
.

3.3
Wavefunctions to resummed amplitudes

We finally compute the
T
-matrix by using the radiation potential (
3.13
) and non-perturbative wavefunctions (
3.36
). We come back to the natural units
\hbar=1
to simplify the notation. We use the factorisations (
3.18
) and (
3.22
) and work in the COM frame of the initial state
\bm{P}=0
for simplicity. The
T
-matrix of the one-graviton emission process is then written by

\displaystyle T_{\beta,\alpha}

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm BO}}{=}\sqrt{32E_{1}E_{2}E^{\prime}_{1}E^{\prime}_{2}\omega}\braket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}};\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\hat{R}_{\rm PM}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{P}^{\prime}+\bm{k})\,.

(3.41)

The kernel of the
T
-matrix is thus the following integrals

\displaystyle\braket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}};\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\hat{R}_{\rm PM}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\ell}\braket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\bm{\ell}}\,\mathcal{R}

(3.42)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle:=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\rho}\,\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\rho}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}[\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})]^{*}e^{i\bm{\rho}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{x}^{\prime}}R_{\rm PM}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime},\bm{\ell},\bm{\rho})e^{-i\bm{\rho}\cdot\bm{x}}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})\,.

(3.43)

Here,
R_{\rm PM}
is the momentum-space radiation potential originally introduced in (
3.13
). Using (
3.18
) and (
3.21
) with the change of notation
\bm{p}\to\bm{\rho}
and
\bm{k}\to\bm{\ell}
, it is given by

\displaystyle\braket{\bm{P}^{\prime}+\bm{\ell};\bm{\rho}^{\prime};\bm{\ell}|\hat{R}_{\rm PM}|\bm{P}=\bm{0};\bm{\rho}}=\hat{\rm\delta}(\bm{P}^{\prime}+\bm{\ell})R_{\rm PM}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime},\bm{\ell},\bm{\rho})\,.

(3.44)

We define the position-space radiation potential by
12
12

12

We define it for the outgoing momentum
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}
. Instead, one can define it for the average momentum
\frac{1}{2}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime}+\bm{\rho})
to keep the symmetry between the in and out momenta, and follow similar steps to (
3.27
).

\displaystyle R_{\rm PM}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime},\bm{\ell},\bm{x}):=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}\,e^{-i\bm{q}\cdot\bm{x}}R_{\rm PM}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime},\bm{\ell},\bm{\rho})|_{\bm{\rho}=\bm{\rho}^{\prime}+\bm{q}}

(3.45)

so that the integral (
3.43
) can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\rho}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}[\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})]^{*}e^{i\bm{\rho}^{\prime}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}R_{\rm PM}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime},\bm{\ell},\bm{x})\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})\,.

(3.46)

We assume that the position-space radiation potential has no super-classical part, that is, it is regular in the classical limit
\hbar\to 0
.
The integrals of
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}
and
\bm{x}
can then be performed by applying the saddle-point approximation. The rapidly oscillating parts of (
3.46
) come from
e^{i\bm{\rho}^{\prime}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})\propto e^{i\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}
. Therefore, the saddle is

\displaystyle\bm{x}=\bm{x}^{\prime}\,,\qquad\bm{\rho}^{\prime}=\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})=\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x})\,.

(3.47)

By relabelling
\bm{x}^{\prime}\to\bm{x}
to simplify the notation, we finally obtain

5-point amplitude kernel

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle=\int{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}[\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})]^{*}R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}),\bm{\ell},\bm{x})\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})\,.

(3.48)

Eqs. (
3.41
), (
3.42
), and (
3.48
) are our general integral expressions to compute the non-perturbative 5-point amplitude under the WKB approximation. They are valid for the generic two-body potential and radiation potential to all orders in
G
under the WKB approximation and the factorisation approximation of the three-particle state (
3.22
).

We conclude this section by providing the explicit form of the position-space radiation potential. In general, classically relevant contributions of
R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{k},\bm{x})
are expected to arise from singularities of the momentum-space potential in the momentum transfer
\bm{q}
. At the leading order (
3.17
), the singularities are the delta functions. In the classical limit, the outgoing momenta are approximated by

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}=\bm{p}^{\prime}+\eta_{1}(\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime})\bm{P}^{\prime}\simeq\bm{p}^{\prime}-\eta_{1}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})\bm{k}\,,~~\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}=-\bm{p}^{\prime}+\eta_{2}(\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime})\bm{P}^{\prime}\simeq-\bm{p}^{\prime}-\eta_{2}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})\bm{k}\,,

(3.49)

where we recall that the two-body out state is no longer in the COM frame due to the momentum conservation
\bm{P}^{\prime}+\bm{k}=\bm{P}=0
. Hence, the momentum transfer of each particle is

\displaystyle\bm{q}_{1}:=\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}\simeq\bm{q}+\eta_{1}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})\bm{k}\,,\qquad\bm{q}_{2}:=\bm{p}_{2}-\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}\simeq-\bm{q}+\eta_{2}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})\bm{k}\,.

(3.50)

Then, the
\bm{q}
integral in (
3.45
) is trivial thanks to the delta functions in (
3.17
), yielding

Leading-order radiation potential in position space

\displaystyle R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{k},\bm{x})=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2\omega}}\left[e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bar{\bm{x}}_{1}}\frac{(\varepsilon\cdot p^{\prime}_{1})^{2}}{2E_{1}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})}+e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bar{\bm{x}}_{2}}\frac{(\varepsilon\cdot p^{\prime}_{2})^{2}}{2E_{2}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{3})

(3.51)

in the classical limit. The denominator of each term has been simplified by
E_{a}\simeq E_{a}^{\prime}
, and the barred coordinates

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{x}}_{1}=\eta_{2}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})\bm{x}\,,\qquad\bar{\bm{x}}_{2}=-\eta_{1}(\bm{p}^{\prime}{}^{2})\bm{x}

(3.52)

are the relative coordinates of particle
a
from the COM position
\bm{X}
.

4
Waveform from generic conservative dynamics

In Sec.
3
, we have developed a resummation of the 5-point amplitude by applying the twice-projection of Feshbach. The framework is general and does not require specific assumptions about the potentials as long as the WKB approximation and the factorisation approximation are valid. From now on, we ignore the imaginary parts in the potentials, especially the one in the two-body potential
V_{\rm PM}
, and focus on GW emissions from the conservative motion of two bodies. More precisely, we approximate that the two-body potential is Hermitian
\hat{V}_{\rm PM}=\hat{V}_{\rm PM}^{\dagger}
, which reads

\displaystyle V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{p};\bm{P})=V^{*}_{\rm PM}(\bm{p},\bm{p}^{\prime};\bm{P})

(4.1)

in the momentum space, and

\displaystyle V_{\rm PM}(\bar{\bm{p}},\bm{x};\bm{P})=V^{*}_{\rm PM}(\bar{\bm{p}},\bm{x};\bm{P})

(4.2)

in the position space. Nonetheless, except for this additional approximation, the following formulae remain all orders in
G
and are applicable to generic conservative dynamics.

4.1
Waveform as in-in expectation value

Figure 4
:
The initial kinematics of the KMOC formalism.

We calculate gravitational waveforms by using the KMOC formalism
[
34
,
35
]
. To deal with the classical two-body problem, the initial state is chosen to be a wavepacket state so that both position and momentum are well-defined in the classical limit; in the interaction picture, our initial state is

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi}:=\int d\Phi(p_{1},p_{2})\phi_{1}(p_{1})\phi_{2}(p_{2})e^{i(b_{1}\cdot p_{1}+b_{2}\cdot p_{2})}\ket{p_{1};p_{2}}\,.

(4.3)

The wavefunctions
\phi_{a}
are normalized to unity

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}\Phi(p_{a})|\phi_{a}(p_{a})|^{2}=1\,,

(4.4)

and assumed to be sharply peaked at a classical value of the momentum of the body. They are shifted by the translation operators
e^{i(b_{1}\cdot p_{1}+b_{2}\cdot p_{2})}
with spatial vectors
b_{a}
, and

\displaystyle b:=b_{2}-b_{1}

(4.5)

is the impact parameter. See Fig.
4
for illustration.

Classical observables at future infinity are expectation values of operators after time evolution governed by the
S
-matrix operator
\hat{S}
. The waveform is the expectation value of the graviton operator at future null infinity, spanned by the retarded time
u
and the unit vector
\bm{n}
directing to a point on the sphere, so given by
[
35
]

h_{\mu\nu}(u,\bm{n})=\frac{\kappa}{4\pi r}\sum_{\sigma}\int\hat{\rm d}\omega\,\varepsilon^{-\sigma}_{\mu\nu}e^{-i\omega u}W^{\sigma}(k)|_{k=(\omega,\,\omega\bm{n})}+{\rm c.c.},

(4.6)

with

\displaystyle iW^{\sigma}(k)

\displaystyle:=\braket{\Psi|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}^{\sigma}\hat{S}|\Psi}\,,

(4.7)

being the spectral waveform. Here,
a^{\sigma}_{k}
is the annihilation operator of a graviton with the polarisation
\sigma=\pm
and
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}^{\sigma}(k)
is the polarisation tensor. In what follows, the polarisation
\sigma
is implicit as it is insignificant. Under the appropriate choice of the wavefunction satisfying the so-called Goldilocks condition, the spectral waveform in the classical limit is

\displaystyle iW(k)

\displaystyle=\int\left[\prod_{a=1,2}\hat{\rm d}^{4}q_{a}\hat{\rm\delta}(2p_{a}\cdot q_{a})e^{ib_{a}\cdot q_{a}}\right]\braket{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}\,,

(4.8)

where
q_{a}
is the momentum mismatch of the plane waves composed of the initial wavepacket state
q_{a}:=p_{a}-p^{\prime}_{a}
and
p_{a}
are identified with the classical on-shell momenta of the initial two bodies. The
q_{a}
integrals arise from the initial state being the wavepacket, whose domain is restricted to be the on-shell momenta of
p_{a}^{\prime}=p_{a}-q_{a}
as guaranteed by the delta functions, and to be
q_{a}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
because the wavepacket must be supported only around the classical momentum
p_{a}
.

Note that, with
p_{a}^{2}=m_{a}^{2}
, the on-shell condition of
p_{a}^{\prime}
is written as
2p_{a}\cdot q_{a}-q_{a}^{2}=0
. We have ignored
q_{a}^{2}
by the scaling
q_{a}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
. Alternatively, one can relate the barred variable
\bar{p}_{a}=(p_{a}+p_{a}^{\prime})/2
to the classical four-velocity
u_{a}
via
\bar{p}_{a}=\bar{m}_{a}u_{a}
with
\bar{m}_{a}^{2}=m_{a}^{2}-q_{a}^{2}/4
to keep the exact form of the on-shell condition
2\bar{p}_{a}\cdot q_{a}=0
as in
[
39
,
40
]
. This subtlety does not enter our computation because the super-classical parts are manifestly cancelled in the waveform computations, thanks to the resummed form of the wavefunction. We also compute the impulse in Appendix
B
, and show that a non-perturbative impulse can be obtained even if
q_{a}^{2}
is ignored.

Therefore, in the KMOC formalism, the kernel of the waveform is the inclusive amplitude

\displaystyle\braket{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}\,.

(4.9)

The inclusive amplitude (
4.9
) can be rewritten in terms of in-out amplitudes by inserting the completeness relation. In our context, the conservative dynamics refers to discarding the imaginary part of the potentials. The imaginary part of the two-body potential should arise from cutting the amplitudes in terms of on-shell inelastic states. Hence, at the level of conservative dynamics, the relevant on-shell contributions would be the elastic states only, which motivate us to truncate the completeness relation up to the elastic channel:
13
13

13

We also ignore bound states in the completeness relation because in the classical limit the scattering state does not evolve into a bound state at the conservative level.

\displaystyle\hat{1}\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\int{\rm d}\Phi(p_{1},p_{2})\ket{p_{1};p_{2}}\bra{p_{1};p_{2}}\,,

(4.10)

where
\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}
denotes equalities under our assumption on the conservative dynamics.
Then, the inclusive amplitude is

\displaystyle\braket{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}

\displaystyle\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\int{\rm d}\Phi(p^{\prime\prime}_{1},p^{\prime\prime}_{2})\braket{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}|p^{\prime\prime}_{1};p^{\prime\prime}_{2}}\braket{{p^{\prime\prime}_{1};p^{\prime\prime}_{2}|\hat{a}_{k}\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}}

\displaystyle\,=\int{\rm d}\Phi(p^{\prime\prime}_{1},p^{\prime\prime}_{2})\braket{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}|p^{\prime\prime}_{1};p^{\prime\prime}_{2}}\braket{{p^{\prime\prime}_{1};p^{\prime\prime}_{2};k|\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}}\,.

(4.11)

Let us use the results from Sec.
3
. Using (
3.18
) and (
3.22
), the 2-to-2 and 2-to-3
S
-matrix elements are written as

\displaystyle\braket{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}|p_{1};p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{16E^{\prime}_{1}E^{\prime}_{2}E_{1}E_{2}}\,\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{P}^{\prime}-\bm{P})\braket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}|\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}}}\,,

(4.12)

\displaystyle\braket{{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2};k|\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}}

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm BO}}{=}-i\sqrt{32E_{1}E_{2}E^{\prime}_{1}E^{\prime}_{2}\omega}\,\hat{\rm\delta}^{(4)}(P^{\prime}+k-P)\braket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}};\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\hat{R}_{\rm PM}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\,,

(4.13)

with
P^{\mu}=(E_{12},\bm{P})
and
k^{\mu}=(\omega,\bm{k})
. Here, we write the 2-to-2
S
-matrix in the form of the inner product of in and out states. In (
4.13
), we combined the energy delta function in (
2.3
) with the momentum delta function in (
3.41
). We then use the fact that the interacting states also satisfy the completeness relation for the conservative dynamics

\displaystyle\hat{1}\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}\ket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}}}\bra{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}}}\,.

(4.14)

As a result, after performing the total momentum integral, the inclusive amplitude under both the classical limit and the conservative dynamics is given by

In-in formula of inclusive amplitude

\displaystyle\braket{p^{\prime}_{1};p^{\prime}_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{\rm BO}{\underset{\rm cons}{=}}-i\sqrt{32E_{1}E_{2}E^{\prime}_{1}E^{\prime}_{2}\omega}\,\hat{\rm\delta}^{(4)}(P^{\prime}+k-P)\braket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}};\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\hat{R}_{\rm PM}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\,.

(4.15)

This expression is quite close to that of the 5-point
S
-matrix (
4.13
), but the out state of the two bodies is replaced with the in state. In fact, Refs.
[
42
,
82
]
pointed out that the inclusive amplitude is related to the 5-point amplitude via analytic continuation, which changes the sign of
i\varepsilon
of the out two bodies to opposite. Our formulae (
4.13
) and (
4.15
) indeed suggest such a structure as they are related by
\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}\leftrightarrow\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}
. Note also that the expression analogous to (
4.15
) was obtained in the external mass-ratio limit
[
68
]
(see also
[
64
]
for the earlier development). In this case,
\Psi^{\pm}_{\bm{p}}
and
\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}
are given by the solutions to the Klein-Gordon equations in the Schwarzschild background under the in/out boundary conditions. However, we stress that the formula (
4.15
) is derived under generic assumptions of the classical limit and the conservative dynamics to all orders in
G
, without any assumption of the mass ratio.

It would be worth mentioning at this stage how the analysis above should be corrected in non-conservative dynamics. First of all, we should include additional contributions in the completeness relation (
4.10
). In fact, it is well-known that the computation of the impulse with the radiation reaction requires additional cut contributions in the KMOC formalism
[
34
]
. Second, a non-Hermitian system is biorthogonal, requiring dual states to form a complete set
[
71
]
. Therefore, (
4.14
) does not hold, invalidating to rewrite of the inclusive amplitude into the simple in-in form (
4.15
). Having said that, the results in Sec.
3
hold even in non-conservative systems with non-Hermitian potentials. It would be interesting to get a more systematic understanding of the non-conservative case, which we leave for future study.

4.2
Resummed waveform

Collecting all our developments, let’s compute the KMOC waveform:

\displaystyle W\overset{\rm BO}{\underset{\rm cons}{=}}-\int

\displaystyle\hat{\rm d}^{4}q_{1}\hat{\rm d}^{4}q_{2}\hat{\rm\delta}(2p_{1}\cdot q_{1})\hat{\rm\delta}(2p_{2}\cdot q_{2})\hat{\rm\delta}^{(4)}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)e^{i(b_{1}\cdot q_{1}+b_{2}\cdot q_{2})}

\displaystyle\times 4E_{1}E_{2}\sqrt{2\omega}\braket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}-\bm{q}};\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\hat{R}_{\rm PM}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\,,

(4.16)

where we have simplified the overall factor of (
4.15
) under the classical limit
E_{a}\simeq E^{\prime}_{a}
and rewritten the delta functions for the four-momentum conservation in terms of the momentum mismatches
q_{a}=p_{a}-p^{\prime}_{a}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
.
We introduce the following variables

\displaystyle p_{1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}P+p\,,\qquad p_{2}=\eta_{2}P-p\,,

(4.17)

\displaystyle q_{1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}Q+q\,,\qquad q_{2}=\eta_{2}Q-q\,,

(4.18)

and change the integration variables from
q_{a}
to
q
and
Q
. We work in the COM frame with the choice (
3.38
), where the total and relative four-momenta are

\displaystyle P^{\mu}=(E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2}),\bm{0})\,,\qquad p^{\mu}=(0,\bm{p})\,.

(4.19)

The vectors
b_{a}
are purely spatial
b_{a}=(0,\bm{b}_{a})
. We perform the
Q
integral and the
q^{0}
integral by using the delta functions of the conservation and one of the on-shell conditions, yielding

\displaystyle W\overset{\rm BO}{\underset{\rm cons}{=}}-\sqrt{2\omega}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}\,\hat{\rm\delta}\left(\omega-\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})\cdot\bm{q}\right)e^{-i\bm{b}\cdot\bm{q}-i\bm{X}_{0}\cdot\bm{k}}\braket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}-\bm{q}};\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\hat{R}_{\rm PM}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}

(4.20)

where

\displaystyle\bm{b}:=\bm{b}_{1}-\bm{b}_{2}\,,\qquad\bm{X}_{0}=\eta_{1}\bm{b}_{1}+\eta_{2}\bm{b}_{2}

(4.21)

are the impact parameter and the initial position of the COM, respectively.

The inclusive amplitude (
4.15
) is computed in the same manner as Sec.
3.3
. The spectral waveform is then

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\overset{\rm BO}{\underset{\rm cons}{=}}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\ell}\braket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}|\bm{\ell}}\mathcal{T}(\bm{\ell})\,,

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\bm{\ell})

\displaystyle:=-\sqrt{2\omega}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}\,\hat{\rm\delta}\left(\omega-\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})\cdot\bm{q}\right)e^{-i\bm{b}\cdot\bm{q}-i\bm{X}_{0}\cdot\bm{k}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times[\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}-\bm{q}}(\bm{x})]^{*}R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}),\bm{\ell},\bm{x})\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})\,.

(4.23)

Alternatively, by using the wavefunction of the graviton
\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}(\bm{y})=\braket{\bm{y}|\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}}
, we obtain

Resummed waveform

\displaystyle W\overset{\rm BO}{\underset{\rm cons}{=}}\int{\rm d}^{3}\bm{y}[\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}(\bm{y})]^{*}\mathcal{T}(\bm{y})\,,\quad\mathcal{T}(\bm{y})=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{\ell}e^{-i\bm{\ell}\cdot\bm{y}}\,\mathcal{T}(\bm{\ell})\,.

(4.24)

By comparing the classical computation of the waveform, the
\bm{y}
integral in (
4.24
) can be understood as the integral of the product of the position-space Green’s function and the source term
\mathcal{T}(\bm{y})
. See Sec. 4 of
[
68
]
for a comparison between classical and amplitude computations in the context of black hole perturbation. Therefore, the wavefunction
\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}(\bm{y})
is expected to be responsible for the propagation effect, and the generation of GWs should be described by
\mathcal{T}
.

Let us proceed with calculating the source term
\mathcal{T}(\bm{\ell})
. Our framework is based on time-independent scattering theory, where there has been no time explicitly. The time naturally emerges from the remaining on-shell delta function:

\displaystyle\hat{\rm\delta}\left(\omega-\bm{q}\cdot\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})\right)=\int{\rm d}t\,e^{i\omega t-i\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})\cdot\bm{q}\,t}\,.

(4.25)

Using the WKB wavefunction (
3.36
) with care of the deviation from the COM frame, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\bm{\ell})

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2\omega}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}t\,e^{i\omega t}e^{-i\bm{X}_{0}\cdot\bm{k}-i\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}}e^{-i(\bm{b}+\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})t-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}})\cdot\bm{q}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times|\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})|\,R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}),\bm{\ell},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2\omega}\int{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}t\,e^{i\omega t}e^{-i\bm{X}_{0}\cdot\bm{k}-i\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}}\delta^{3}(\bm{b}+\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})t-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times|\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})|\,R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}),\bm{\ell},\bm{x})

(4.26)

where we used
\bm{q}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
and ignored terms of
\mathcal{O}(\bm{q}^{2})
in the exponent. The super-classical piece of the WKB wavefunction is cancelled thanks to the in-in form of the inclusive amplitude. We notice that the argument of the delta function,

\displaystyle\bm{b}+\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})t-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})=0\,,

(4.27)

is precisely the equation for the classical trajectory in the Hamilton-Jacobi formalism. Hence, denoting its solution by
\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\delta^{(3)}(\bm{b}+\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}(\bm{p}^{2})t-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}))=\frac{\delta^{(3)}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))}{|\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{\rm COM}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})|}\,,

(4.28)

and then

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\bm{\ell})

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2\omega}\int{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}t\,e^{i\omega t}e^{-i\bm{X}_{0}\cdot\bm{k}-i\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}}\delta^{(3)}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}),\bm{\ell},\bm{x})\,.

(4.29)

We finally use the support of the classical trajectory
\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t)
to rewrite
\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}
. We add the subscript “cl” when quantities are evaluated on the trajectory,

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{\rm cl}(t):=\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))\,,\qquad\bm{V}_{\rm cl}(t):=\bm{V}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))\,,

(4.30)

to which we can apply Hamilton’s equations. Eq. (
3.39
) with the replacement
\bm{P}\to-\bm{k}
can then be written by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\,\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))}{\partial\bm{x}_{\rm cl}}\cdot\frac{{\rm d}\bm{x}_{\rm cl}}{{\rm d}t}-\bm{V}_{\rm cl}\cdot\bm{k}=\frac{{\rm d}\,\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))}{{\rm d}t}-\bm{V}_{\rm cl}\cdot\bm{k}=0\,,

(4.31)

We find

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{0}\cdot\bm{k}+\delta\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))=\left(\bm{X}_{0}+\int^{t}_{-\infty}{\rm d}t^{\prime}\bm{V}_{\rm cl}(t^{\prime})\right)\cdot\bm{k}=\bm{X}_{\rm cl}(t)\cdot\bm{k}

(4.32)

with
\bm{X}_{\rm cl}(t)
being the position of the COM at time
t
. All in all, our final formula is

Source term of gravitational waves

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\bm{\ell})=-\sqrt{2\omega}\int{\rm d}t\ e^{i\omega t}e^{-i\bm{X}_{\rm cl}(t)\cdot\bm{k}}R_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}_{\rm cl}(t),\bm{\ell},\bm{x}_{\rm cl}(t))\,.

(4.33)

This is our main result.

Let us use the leading order radiation potential (
3.51
) to see the waveform more concretely. Assuming the free propagation
\ket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}}=\ket{\bm{k}}
, we immediately find

\displaystyle W(k)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{a=1,2}\int{\rm d}te^{i\omega t}e^{-i(\bar{\bm{x}}_{a,{\rm cl}}(t)+\bm{X}_{\rm cl}(t))\cdot\bm{k}}\frac{(\varepsilon\cdot p_{a,{\rm cl}}(t))^{2}}{E_{a,{\rm cl}}(t)}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{3})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{a=1,2}\int{\rm d}te^{i\omega t}e^{-i\bm{x}_{a,{\rm cl}}(t)\cdot\bm{k}}\frac{(\varepsilon\cdot p_{a,{\rm cl}}(t))^{2}}{E_{a,{\rm cl}}(t)}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{3})

(4.34)

which is nothing but the Fourier transform of the energy-momentum tensors of worldlines

\displaystyle\int{\rm d}te^{i\omega t}e^{-i\bm{x}_{a,{\rm cl}}(t)\cdot\bm{k}}\frac{(\varepsilon\cdot p_{a,{\rm cl}}(t))^{2}}{E_{a,{\rm cl}}(t)}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}(k)\int{\rm d}^{4}ze^{ik\cdot z}\sum_{a=1,2}T_{a}^{\mu\nu}(z)\,,

(4.35)

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}_{a}(z)

\displaystyle=\delta^{(3)}(\bm{z}-\bm{x}_{a,{\rm cl}}(t))\frac{p^{\mu}_{a,{\rm cl}}p^{\nu}_{a,{\rm cl}}}{E_{a,{\rm cl}}}\,,

(4.36)

where
z^{\mu}=(t,\bm{z})
and
\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}=\varepsilon^{\mu}\varepsilon^{\nu}
. One can see that the positions of the worldlines are correctly pulled back to the coordinate positions
\bm{x}_{a,{\rm cl}}=\bm{X}_{\rm cl}+\bar{\bm{x}}_{a,{\rm cl}}
from the relative positions
\bar{\bm{x}}_{a,{\rm cl}}
to the COM. Hence, we correctly reproduce the gravitational waveform emitted from the generic scattering trajectories of the worldlines under the two-body potential in linearised GWs. Higher-order effects of gravity can be added by extending the radiation potential to higher orders in
G
.

Before concluding, note that our analysis has been based on the initial condition for scattering orbits. However, as we mentioned in footnote
8
, the difference between scattering and bound state wavefunctions is the boundary condition of
\sigma_{\bm{p}}
, so the same analysis can be applied to the bound state wavefunctions. Since our last formula (
4.33
) only requires the classical trajectory of the particles, we can calculate the wave emission from a bound orbit by the same formula (
4.33
) by simply changing the classical trajectory from a scattering orbit to the bound orbit.

5
Conclusion and outlook

In this paper, we have developed a formalism to calculate non-perturbative inelastic scattering amplitudes under the classical limit and applied it to the waveform calculation emitted from conservative two-body dynamics. The idea and main results are summarised in Sec.
1
. To summarise, we have found that the generation of GWs can be computed by integrating the radiation potential along the classical trajectory governed by the post-Minkowskian two-body potential. The radiation potential can be extracted from perturbative scattering amplitudes via Born subtraction. This may be regarded as an extension of the EFT matching of the gravitational potential to radiative processes, formulated through Feshbach’s projector formalism and resummed amplitudes.
There are various directions to develop further, which we list as follows.

•

Higher-order radiation potential.

We have calculated the radiation potential at leading order and confirmed that our formula is equivalent to the GW emission from the energy-momentum tensor of two worldlines. It is natural to extend this calculation to higher orders in
G
, where general-relativistic corrections, e.g., graviton self-interactions, should come in (see the top panel of Fig.
3
). Along this line, it would be interesting to explore if there is a more efficient way to extract the radiation potential or the waveform than using the Born subtraction with the relativistic Hamiltonian, similarly in the case of the two-body dynamics
[
78
,
10
,
79
,
80
,
81
]
.

•

Centre-of-mass dynamics.

As is well known, in general, the centre-of-mass (COM) position is no longer constant in relativistic two-body problems, and its motion also contributes to wave emission. This effect is also evident in our formalism, where COM motion arises from deviations from the COM frame due to the presence of the graviton state. This motivates us to extract information about the COM motion in addition to the relative motion. This is particularly relevant when estimating constants of motion for bound systems, which can be approached using our formalism. Once the COM coordinates are determined, one can readily consider circular orbits and extract key quantities such as their binding energy
[
83
,
84
,
85
]
, which, together with the radiated flux, contribute to the measured phase of gravitational-wave signals.

•

Gravitational wave propagation.

In this paper, we have only implicitly dealt with the graviton wavefunction
\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}
and not discussed the precise justification of the factorisation (
3.22
) nor the equation of motion to determine
\Psi^{-}_{\bm{k}}
. It is desirable to sort out these details. Meanwhile, since our general formula is quite close to the one obtained from the black hole perturbation
[
68
]
, one might be able to approximate it by the solution to the Teukolsky equation, especially if one is interested in black hole mergers commented below. It would also be interesting to use our formalism to compare predictions that Sachs’s peeling property is violated at leading order in the post-Minkowskian expansion, as shown recently in
[
86
]
.

•

Non-conservative dynamics.

The effective potentials of Feshbach are naturally complex and can describe inelastic (non-conservative) effects during scattering processes. In fact, radiation reaction
[
87
,
88
,
89
,
17
,
90
]
, black hole absorption
[
91
,
92
,
93
,
94
,
95
,
96
]
, and even the Hawking radiation
[
97
]
can be understood as the existence of additional inelastic channels.
14
14

14

See also
[
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
]
for the recent developments of other aspects of the amplitudes and Hawking radiation.
This is another advantage to adopt the notion of the effective potential in the Schrödinger equation, instead of using the notion of the classical potential. As we mentioned at the end of Sec.
4.1
, nevertheless, we should modify the derivation of the waveform in the presence of additional channels. It would be intriguing to study whether we can correctly describe the wave emission from non-conservative dynamics by extending the method of this paper.

•

Black hole merger.
Finally, one of the important observables in the current GW experiments is the waveforms from coalescences of two black holes. In the amplitude perspective, the merger is understood as a complete absorption process
[
68
]
, which can be realised by adding an imaginary part to the effective potential at a finite
r
. Also, for the black hole mergers, strong gravity effects should be crucial, requiring a resummation of the effective potentials, i.e., resumming the series expansions in the top panel of Fig.
3
. Since our general formula is valid for all orders in
G
, it may be possible to explore intersections with other strong-gravity methods, such as black hole perturbation/self-force expansion
[
102
,
32
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
31
]
and the effective one-body approach
[
8
,
106
,
107
]
.

Appendix A
Relativistic WKB approximation

This Appendix derives (
3.34
)-(
3.36
) from (
3.31
). A similar analysis can be found in
[
109
,
110
]
for a relativistic Hamiltonian
H_{12}(\bm{p},\bm{x})=\sum_{a}\sqrt{m_{a}^{2}+\bm{p}^{2}}+V(r)
in spherical coordinates. On the other hand, our calculations are performed in Cartesian coordinates rather than spherical coordinates, and applicable to a generic momentum-dependent Hamiltonian
H_{12}(\bm{p},\bm{x})
with the dependence of the total momentum being implicit.

A.1
Saddle-point integrals

Let us start with calculating the integral

\displaystyle I(\hbar)=\int{\rm d}^{n}zF(z)e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}f(z)}\,,

(A.1)

under the saddle-point approximation in higher orders in
\hbar
(see e.g.,
[
111
,
112
,
113
]
). We use the notation like

\displaystyle f_{I}(z)=\frac{\partial f(z)}{\partial z^{I}}\,,\qquad f_{IJ}(z)=\frac{\partial^{2}f(z)}{\partial z^{I}\partial z^{J}}\,,

(A.2)

and denote the saddle by
z_{0}
:

\displaystyle f_{I}(z_{0})=0\,.

(A.3)

We expand the functions around the saddle
z_{0}
,

\displaystyle F(z)

\displaystyle=F(z_{0})+F_{I}(z_{0})w^{I}+\frac{1}{2}F_{IJ}(z_{0})w^{I}w^{J}+\cdots\,,

(A.4)

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle=f(z_{0})+f_{I}(z_{0})w^{I}+\frac{1}{2}f_{IJ}(z_{0})w^{I}w^{J}+\frac{1}{6}f_{IJK}(z_{0})w^{I}w^{J}w^{K}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{24}f_{IJKL}(z_{0})w^{I}w^{J}w^{K}w^{L}+\cdots\,,

(A.5)

where
w:=z-z_{0}
. The integral (
A.1
) can then be written as

\displaystyle I=F(z_{0})e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}f(z_{0})}\int{\rm d}^{n}w\,g(w)\,e^{-\frac{i}{2\hbar}w^{I}f_{IJ}(z_{0})w^{J}}

(A.6)

which we can compute by using the Gaussian integrals.

Let
G^{IJ}
be the inverse of the Hessian,
G^{IJ}=f_{IJ}^{-1}
. Using the shorthand

\displaystyle\langle g(w)\rangle=\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\right)^{n/2}\sqrt{\det f_{ij}}\int{\rm d}^{n}w\,g(w)\,e^{-\frac{i}{2\hbar}w^{I}f_{IJ}(z_{0})w^{J}}\,,

(A.7)

the Gaussian integrals are given by

\displaystyle\langle 1\rangle

\displaystyle=1\,,

(A.8)

\displaystyle\langle w^{I}w^{J}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar}{i}G^{IJ}\,,

(A.9)

\displaystyle\langle w^{I}w^{J}w^{K}w^{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle w^{I}w^{J}\rangle\langle w^{K}w^{L}\rangle+\langle w^{I}w^{K}\rangle\langle w^{J}w^{L}\rangle+\langle w^{I}w^{L}\rangle\langle w^{J}w^{K}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\hbar}{i}\right)^{2}(G^{IJ}G^{KL}+G^{IK}G^{JL}+G^{IL}G^{JK})\,.

(A.10)

The general case is obtained by the Wick theorem

\displaystyle\langle w^{I_{1}}w^{I_{2}}\cdots w^{I_{2N+1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,,

(A.11)

\displaystyle\langle w^{I_{1}}w^{I_{2}}\cdots w^{I_{2N}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\hbar}{i}\right)^{N}\sum_{\text{all pairing}}G^{I_{\alpha_{1}}J_{\beta_{1}}}\cdots G^{I_{\alpha_{n}}J_{\beta_{n}}}\,.

(A.12)

We compute (
A.1
) up to the sub-leading order in the
\hbar
expansion. This requires to evaluate the function
g(w)
up to the following order

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2F}F_{IJ}w^{I}w^{J}-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left(\frac{1}{6F}F_{I}f_{JKL}+\frac{1}{24}f_{IJKL}\right)w^{I}w^{J}w^{K}w^{L}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{72}f_{IJK}f_{LMN}w^{I}w^{J}w^{K}w^{L}w^{M}w^{N}+\cdots

(A.13)

where
\cdots
are terms that vanish or become higher order in
\hbar
when performing the Gaussian integrals. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2\pi\hbar}{i}\right)^{n/2}\frac{Fe^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}f}}{\sqrt{\det f_{IJ}}}

\displaystyle\times\Biggl[1-i\hbar\Biggl(\frac{1}{2F}F_{IJ}G^{IJ}-\frac{1}{2F}F_{I}f_{JKL}G^{IJ}G^{KL}-\frac{1}{8}f_{IJKL}G^{IJ}G^{KL}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{12}f_{IJK}f_{LMN}G^{IL}G^{JM}G^{KN}+\frac{1}{8}f_{IJK}f_{LMN}G^{IJ}G^{LM}G^{KN}\Biggl)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})\Biggl].

(A.14)

A.2
Relativistic Schrödinger equation

We apply the general result (
A.14
) to the integral form of the Schrödinger equation; that is,
z=(\bm{x}^{\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime})
and

\displaystyle F=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{3}}\left[1+\frac{i\hbar}{2}\partial_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}+\cdots\right]H_{12}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{x}^{\prime})\,,\qquad f=\bm{p}^{\prime}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})-\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,.

(A.15)

The Hessian and its inverse are given by

\displaystyle f_{IJ}=\begin{pmatrix}-\partial^{2}_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}&\bm{1}\\
\bm{1}&\bm{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad G^{IJ}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}&\bm{1}\\
\bm{1}&\partial^{2}_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\,.

(A.16)

Therefore, the saddle-point approximation of (
3.31
) gives

\displaystyle E_{12}-H_{12}+i\hbar\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}\partial p^{\prime}_{j}}\frac{\partial^{2}\sigma_{\bm{p}}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}\partial x^{i}}+\frac{1}{A_{\bm{p}}}\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}}\frac{\partial A_{\bm{p}}}{\partial x^{i}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2})=0\,,

(A.17)

under
\bm{p}^{\prime}=\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})=\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x})
. In the following, we keep using
\bm{p}^{\prime}
(and set
\hbar=1
) for notational simplicity, but it is understood that it is evaluated at
\bm{p}^{\prime}=\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x})
.

We define the phase
\sigma_{\bm{p}}
as the solution to the leading part of (
A.17
) in order to split it into a set of equations

\displaystyle E_{12}(\bm{p})-H_{12}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=0\,,

(A.18)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}\partial p^{\prime}_{j}}\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}}{\partial x^{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}\partial x^{i}}+\frac{1}{A_{\bm{p}}}\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}}\frac{\partial A_{\bm{p}}}{\partial x^{i}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(A.19)

where
p^{\prime}_{i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}}{\partial x^{j}}=\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{j}}{\partial x^{i}}=\frac{\partial^{2}\sigma_{\bm{p}}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}}\,.

(A.20)

Eq. (
A.19
) can be written in the form of the continuity equation (
3.35
) by introducing the bulk velocity
\bm{v}_{\rm bulk}:=\left.\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial\bm{p}}\right|_{\bm{p}=\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{x})}
. Hence, Eqs. (
A.18
) and (
A.19
) are the well-known set of equations under the WKB approximation, but are valid for a generic relativistic Hamiltonian
H_{12}=H_{12}(\bm{p},\bm{x})
.

To solve (
A.19
), we use the following identities obtained by differentiating (
A.18
):

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}p_{i}}\eqref{WKB_Sch1}=0~

\displaystyle\implies~\frac{\partial E_{12}}{\partial p_{i}}-\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{j}}\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{j}}{\partial p_{i}}=0\,,

(A.21)

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}p_{i}{\rm d}x^{j}}\eqref{WKB_Sch1}=0~

\displaystyle\implies~\left(\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}{\partial x^{j}}+\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}\partial x^{j}}\right)\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}}{\partial p_{i}}+\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}}\frac{\partial^{2}p^{\prime}_{k}}{\partial p_{i}\partial x^{j}}=0\,.

(A.22)

Multiplying the inverse
\left(\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{j}}{\partial p_{i}}\right)^{-1}
, the second equation yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}{\partial x^{j}}+\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}\partial x^{j}}+\left(\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}}{\partial p_{i}}\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}\frac{\partial^{2}p^{\prime}_{l}}{\partial p_{i}\partial x^{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}{\partial x^{j}}+\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}\partial x^{j}}+\left(\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{k}}{\partial p_{i}}\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{l}}\frac{\partial^{2}p^{\prime}_{j}}{\partial p_{i}\partial x^{l}}\,.

(A.23)

Then, its trace can be written by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}\partial p^{\prime}_{j}}\frac{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}}{\partial x^{j}}+\frac{\partial^{2}H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}\partial x^{i}}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}}\frac{\partial H_{12}}{\partial p^{\prime}_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\sqrt{\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}=0\,,

(A.24)

which exactly agrees with (
A.19
) under
A_{\bm{p}}\propto\sqrt{\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}
. The proportional constant is fixed to be unity by imposing the boundary condition
A_{\bm{p}}\to 1
as
\sigma_{\bm{p}}\to\bm{p}\cdot\bm{x}
.

Note that the analysis so far does not require the Hamiltonian to be Hermitian. Let us now assume a Hermitian Hamiltonian (conservative system) and confirm that the wavefunction satisfies completeness

\displaystyle\hat{1}\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}\ket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\bra{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\delta^{(3)}(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}[\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})]^{*}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})

(A.25)

and orthogonality

\displaystyle\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}^{\prime}-\bm{p})\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\braket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{p}^{\prime}-\bm{p})\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\int{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}[\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})]^{*}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})\,,

(A.26)

within the WKB approximation. We only compute the former, as the latter can be verified similarly. Due to the rapid oscillation of the wavefunction, the integral
\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}[\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})]^{*}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})
must have support only around
\bm{x}\simeq\bm{x}^{\prime}
. Assuming
\sigma_{\bm{p}}
is real, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}[\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})]^{*}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\simeq\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}|A_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})|^{2}e^{-i\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}\frac{|A_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})|^{2}}{|\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})|}e^{-i\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\,.

(A.27)

Hence, one sees that
A_{\bm{p}}=\sqrt{\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}
yields the correct normalisation of the wavefunction. Conversely, by imposing (
A.25
), one can obtain
A_{\bm{p}}=\sqrt{\det\partial_{\bm{p}}\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})}
without going through evaluating the saddle-point integral up to the sub-leading order, although this cannot be applied to the non-Hermitian case.

Finally, we briefly comment on the role of the derivative terms in the integral form of the Schrödinger equation (
3.29
). They can be understood as arising from the non-commutativity of momenta and positions, which becomes manifest by moving the integral form to the differential form. Note that this is different from canonical quantisation, where there is a well-known ambiguity of the ordering of
\bm{p}
and
\bm{x}
when they are promoted to operators. Here, there is no such ambiguity because we just move a representation of the Schrödinger equation to another representation. Let us consider a simple example
V_{\rm PM}=c_{1}\bm{x}\cdot\bm{p}
. In such a case, we obtain

\displaystyle\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bm{p}^{\prime}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\left[1+\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}+\cdots\right]V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=c_{1}\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bm{p}^{\prime}\cdot(\bm{x}^{\prime}-\bm{x})}\left[\hat{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\cdot\bm{x}^{\prime}+\frac{i}{2}\right]\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}}{2}\left[\bm{x}\cdot\hat{\bm{p}}+\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{x}\right]\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})

(A.28)

with
\hat{\bm{p}}^{\prime}=-i\partial_{\bm{x}^{\prime}}
and
\hat{\bm{p}}=-i\partial_{\bm{x}}
. We correctly get the self-adjoint potential
\frac{c_{1}}{2}(\bm{x}\cdot\hat{\bm{p}}+\hat{\bm{p}}\cdot\bm{x})
thanks to the derivative term on the first line.

Appendix B
All order impulse for conservative dynamics

In this section, we compute the impulse

\displaystyle\Delta p_{1}^{\mu}:=\braket{\Psi|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{1}^{\mu}\hat{S}|\Psi}-\braket{\Psi|\hat{P}_{1}^{\mu}|\Psi}

(B.1)

and confirm the agreement with classical physics for a generic conservative two-body potential. Recall that conservative dynamics implies (i) the two-body potential
V_{\rm PM}(\bm{p},\bm{x})
and the WKB phase
\sigma_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})
are real and (ii) the completeness relation can be truncated up to the two-particle state (
4.10
). For simplicity, we work in the COM frame and focus on the spatial component. Using the truncated completeness relation and taking the classical limit, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta\bm{p}

\displaystyle=\bm{p}_{f}-\bm{p}_{i}\,,

(B.2)

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{f}

\displaystyle\underset{{\rm cons}}{=}\int\left[\prod_{a=1,2}\hat{\rm d}^{4}q_{a}\hat{\rm\delta}(2p_{a}\cdot q_{a})e^{ib_{a}\cdot q_{a}}\right]

\displaystyle\times\int{\rm d}\Phi(p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime})\bm{p}^{\prime}\braket{p_{1}-q_{1};p_{2}-q_{2}|\hat{S}^{\dagger}|p_{1}^{\prime};p_{2}^{\prime}}\braket{p_{1}^{\prime};p_{2}^{\prime}|\hat{S}|p_{1};p_{2}}\,,

(B.3)

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bm{p}\,.

(B.4)

Note that we ignore the
q^{2}
term in the on-shell delta functions of (
B.3
). This should not be confused with the statement in
[
51
]
that the
q^{2}
term is necessary to compute the impulse beyond the leading order. In our case,
q
is the
momentum mismatch
of the in momenta of the wavepackets, while
q
in
[
51
]
is the
momentum transfer
between the in and out momenta. We assume
q_{a}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
, but do not assume
p_{a}-p_{a}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
consistently with
[
51
]
.

Using the interacting states and the variables introduced in (
4.17
) and (
4.18
), the final momentum is

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{f}

\displaystyle=\int\left[\prod_{a=1,2}\hat{\rm d}^{4}q_{a}\hat{\rm\delta}(2p_{a}\cdot q_{a})e^{ib_{a}\cdot q_{a}}\right]4E_{1}E_{2}\,\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{Q})\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}\,\bm{p}^{\prime}\braket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}-\bm{q}}|\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}}\braket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}e^{-i\bm{b}\cdot\bm{q}}\bm{p}^{\prime}\braket{\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}-\bm{q}}|\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}}\braket{\Psi^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}|\Psi^{+}_{\bm{p}}}\,.

(B.5)

We have performed
q^{0},Q^{0},
and
\bm{Q}
integrals with the help of the two on-shell delta functions
\hat{\rm\delta}(2p_{a}\cdot q_{a})
and the delta function for spatial conservation
\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{Q})=\hat{\rm\delta}^{(3)}(\bm{q}_{1}+\bm{q}_{2})
. Note that there is only the spatial delta function on the first line because the energy conservation is hidden in the interacting states (see (
2.3
)). It can be compared with the waveform (
4.20
), where one on-shell delta function remains, generating the time integral. Their difference can be understood by the distinct nature of impulse and waveform: the impulse is, by definition, only sensitive to the initial and final momenta, whereas the waveform is sensitive to the detailed bulk dynamics of massive bodies. For the former, we don’t need time if the scattering data is given, which, in our case, is encoded in the wavefunctions.

Let’s make it more concrete. The WKB wavefunction gives

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{f}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bm{b}\cdot\bm{q}}\bm{p}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times A^{*}_{\bm{p}-\bm{q}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})A_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})A^{*}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})A_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})e^{i(\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})+\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})-\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}-\bm{q}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})-\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}))}

\displaystyle\simeq\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{q}\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}e^{-i\bm{q}\cdot(\bm{b}-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime}))}\bm{p}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times A^{*}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})A_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})A^{*}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})A_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})e^{i(\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})+\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})-\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})-\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}))}

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\rm d}^{3}\bm{p}^{\prime}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}{\rm d}^{3}\bm{x}^{\prime}\delta^{(3)}(\bm{b}-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime}))\,\bm{p}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times A^{*}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})A_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})A^{*}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})A_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})e^{i(\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})+\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})-\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})-\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}))}

(B.6)

for small
\bm{q}
. Here, the superscripts of
\sigma^{\pm}_{\bm{p}}
denote the in/out boundary conditions. The delta function localises
\bm{x}^{\prime}
to be
\bm{x}^{\prime}=\bm{b}_{\rm eik}
with
\bm{b}_{\rm eik}
denoting the solution to the delta function. The saddle of
\bm{x}
and
\bm{p}^{\prime}
integrals is

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})=\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})\,,\qquad\partial_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})=\partial_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,,

(B.7)

the latter of which can be solved by
\bm{x}=\bm{x}^{\prime}
. Hence, the saddle-point approximation, together with the support of the delta function, yields

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{f}=\bm{p}^{\prime}

(B.8)

with
\bm{p}^{\prime}
being a solution to

\displaystyle\bm{b}-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{b}_{\rm eik})=0\,,\qquad[\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}(\bm{x})-\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})]_{\bm{x}=\bm{b}_{\rm eik}}=0\,.

(B.9)

To understand that this reproduces the correct classical physics, we identify
\sigma^{\pm}_{\bm{p}}
with Hamilton’s characteristic functions of classical physics. The trajectory equations are

\displaystyle\bm{b}+\partial_{\bm{p}}E_{12}t-\partial_{\bm{p}}\sigma_{\bm{p}}^{\pm}(\bm{x})=0\,.

(B.10)

According to the boundary conditions
\sigma^{\pm}_{\bm{p}}
, the solution for
\pm
corresponds to the classical trajectory with the momentum
\bm{p}
at
t=\mp\infty
and with the impact parameter
\bm{b}
. The first condition in (
B.9
) is thus understood as determining the position of the two particles at
t=0
, and the second one provides the matching condition of the in/out trajectories at
t=0
; the in/out trajectories are at the same position
\bm{x}=\bm{b}_{\rm eik}
and have the same momentum
\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{+}_{\bm{p}}=\bm{p}_{\rm bulk}(\bm{b}_{\rm eik})=\partial_{\bm{x}}\sigma^{-}_{\bm{p}^{\prime}}
at
t=0
. As a result, the solution
\bm{p}^{\prime}
to (
B.9
) agrees with the final momentum of the two-body dynamics for the initial momentum
\bm{p}
and the impact parameter
\bm{b}
.
